### [Pragmatic FDT, and predictors as game theory](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SdGbWkCZgCN7EGBxM/pragmatic-fdt-and-predictors-as-game-theory-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Decision theory is back in fashion (defining fashion as "one good post on a good EA blog"). Bentham's Bulldog (BB) has <a href="https://benthams.substack.com/p/functional-decision-theory-not-even">published a case</a> against FDT (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060">functional decision theory</a>), contrasting rationalist enthusiasm with academic scepticism: "<em>Academic decision theorists don't like the theory. The number of academic decision theorists who adopt it could be counted on one hand by someone missing four of their fingers.</em>"</p>
<p>I am, just barely, a published <a href="https://ora.ox.ac.uk/objects/uuid:f1a9fafb-2391-4fdc-99c0-96b63c86ba56">academic decision theorist</a>, so you can keep a small finger to count me too. My position is that, though FDT may have problems with its definitions and under-definedness, we can build defined variants that achieve what FDT attempted to.</p>
<p>I want to do two things in this post. First, sketch a "pragmatic" version of FDT designed to sidestep the theoretical pitfalls that <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ySLYSsNeFL5CoAQzN/a-critique-of-functional-decision-theory">Will MacAskill</a> and <a href="https://www.umsu.de/blog/2018/688">Wolfgang Schwarz</a> identify. Second, take a closer look at what predictors actually do, and argue that whenever they make counterfactual predictions, decision theory shades into game theory -- which explains why EDT/TDT/UDT/FDT can look irrational in the odd branch. It's the old debate of "should you pay the blackmailer", dressed up in predictor garb.</p>
<h1>Pragmatic FDT</h1>
<p>MacAskill and BB both press on the difficulty of saying, formally, whether two algorithms are "the same." Rather than solving that, I'm going to retreat and declare victory. I won't define whether two algorithms are the same in any abstract sense, and I'll ignore logical counterfactuals and counterpossibles entirely. Instead I say that two algorithms are equivalent if the equivalence can be <em>built</em>:</p>
<ul>
<li>
<p><strong>p-FDT:</strong> a pragmatic FDT agent decides in four steps:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Baseline.</strong> Compute the CDT<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> action and its expected utility. This is the default.</li>
<li><strong>Search.</strong> Look for <em>likely-true</em> isomorphisms  between the agent's own  decision process and parts of the world, using the ordinary tools we humans use to judge when two algorithms compute the same function (laid out below).</li>
<li><strong>Evaluate.</strong> For each candidate , find the input-output map  that  maximises expected utility, on the assumption that choosing  as the agent's own decision map also sets  (the isomorphic process  out in the world). Weight by the probability that  is true: with probability , the world responds as the isomorphism dictates; with probability , it behaves as the causal baseline says and the agent has merely played  into a world that ignores it.</li>
<li><strong>Adopt or default.</strong> Call  <em>exploitable</em> if its best  beats the baseline in expected utility. Adopt the highest expected-utility exploitable  found; if none exist, take the baseline CDT action.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ul>
<p>Here, what's an isomorphism between functions? Suppose we have an invertible map  between (sets of) inputs of functions  and , and between (sets of) their outputs. The two functions are isomorphic if . Think of  as relabelling inputs and outputs: it says that, up to relabelling,  and  are the same thing.</p>
<p>Take Will's calculators:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>consider two calculators. The first calculator is like calculators we are used to. The second calculator is from a foreign land: it's identical except that the numbers it outputs always come with a negative sign ('–') in front of them when you'd expect there to be none, and no negative sign when you expect there to be one. Are these calculators running the same algorithm or not?</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Will's answer is that it depends on how you interpret the '–', and there's no fact of the matter. Under p-FDT we don't need one. The calculators are plainly <em>isomorphic</em>:  maps identical inputs to identical inputs, and adds or removes the minus sign on the outputs. Up to that relabelling they compute the same thing. Whatever the foreign calculator was <em>intended</em> to do, it runs an algorithm isomorphic to the standard one, and that's all we'll ever need.</p>
<p>Note that  operates on <em>sets</em> of inputs and outputs, so it's also an <strong>abstraction</strong>. Every way of typing "2+2=" counts as the same input, whether the calculator user is standing on their head, declaiming opera, or has sat down on the calculator in their back pocket. Every "4" (or "–4") counts as the same output, though each corresponds to quadrillions of atoms on the screen in subtly different, moment-to-moment-changing positions. This is the same trick underlying all of computer science (formal abstractions of messy physical processes) and all statistical reasoning. Abstractions are used because they're useful.</p>
<p>A useful abstraction need not cover all situations. The number  is neither even nor odd; the South Pole has no time-zone; wood has no boiling point. So maybe the first calculator has a  key while the foreign one doesn't, and vice versa for the  key. Maybe the user could smash the first calculator on the floor and jump up and down on its ruins, and this has no clear isomorphism to the second calculator.</p>
<p>So  need not be total -- it need not map from or to all inputs and all outputs. And it need not be maximally complex; indeed minimal isomorphisms are often the most useful. When playing the Prisoner's Dilemma against an identical copy of yourself, there is a strong isomorphism between every detail of both your thoughts and actions, but all you really need to know is "we will both cooperate, or both defect".</p>
<h2>Exploitable isomorphisms</h2>
<p>By design,  must be exploitable: so that the agent gains from acting on its existence. In the standard Newcomb problem, that is certainly the case -- making Omega believe the agent will one-box is of great value (the cost being that the agent will actually one-box). So the search for  is not a search for some abstract equivalence between, say, your brain and a roiling cloud of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permutation_City">dust</a> or my brain and the US economy. It needs to be an exploitable isomorphism, where the agent can understand the inputs and outputs and how changes to the input-output map affect the world and hence its own utility. No-one has yet proposed a plausible exploitable isomorphism between my brain and the US economy, or reasons to think that one exists<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<p>For a limited agent there's an extra caveat: the agent must actually be <em>able to implement</em> the winning . In <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060">Parfit's Hitchhiker</a>, it's easy to see there is an isomorphism between 'appear fully trustworthy' and 'get saved by the driver'. But maybe that move is beyond the human hitchhiker. Maybe instead the best isomorphism has an output which is 'become genuinely willing to pay, by focusing on gratitude towards the driver', because that output is actually implementable.</p>
<h2>How we identify likely-true isomorphisms</h2>
<p>So how do we actually find these things? We already have a whole toolkit, and it's worth laying it out, because the reassuring point is that <em>none of it is new metaphysics</em><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span>. It's the ordinary business of deciding when two processes compute the same function, run at whatever level of rigour the stakes demand.</p>
<p>Roughly from cheapest-and-most-certain to most expensive-and-least-certain:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Identity and near-identity.</strong> We can identify an agent with a near-exact copy of itself, and with a faithful simulation of itself. These are the easy cases: the isomorphism is transparent and barely needs checking.</li>
<li><strong>Same code, different substrate.</strong> Two identical pieces of code running on different machines compute the same function, as long as <a href="https://www.joelonsoftware.com/2002/11/11/the-law-of-leaky-abstractions/">the abstraction doesn't leak</a>. Overflow, timing, hardware faults, and side-channels are all ways they can leak; known problems, with known ways of taking them into account.</li>
<li><strong>Different code, same task.</strong> Two different implementations -- a bubble sort and a quicksort, two chess engines that always pick the same move, a compiled and an interpreted version of one program -- can be isomorphic at the level of the input-output map we care about, even when their internals differ wildly.</li>
<li><strong>Coarse behavioural equivalence.</strong> Sometimes we only need a fragment of the map. Two negotiators from the same culture may be isomorphic just over "how they respond to a lowball offer," and nowhere else; two thermostats built by rival firms agree on "switch the heating on below the setpoint" while sharing no circuitry. A partial isomorphism over the decision-relevant slice is enough.</li>
<li><strong>Black-box testing.</strong> When we can't see inside, we probe. Feed a wide variety of inputs from across the input space, and specifically try to make the two processes <em>diverge</em> -- hunt for the input where they come apart. If we can't find one after honest effort, we provisionally treat them as isomorphic over the region we tested.</li>
<li><strong>Vetting a claimed predictor.</strong> If Omega claims a great track record, we check it -- including with randomised trials, to rule out the possibility that Omega is riding a superficial correlation (the gene) rather than tracking our decision process (the simulation). If someone claims to see the future, we subject them to highly sceptical investigation; if someone claims merely to read intent, moderately sceptical investigation. Throughout, we ask whether the purported isomorphism is compatible with everything else we know about the world.</li>
</ul>
<p>We use all the tools that human reasoning and trained common sense make available, and we'll need them: in toy models a simple formal check suffices, but in the messy world, identifying useful isomorphisms is a task of arbitrarily high complexity. Often the agent will find none, and default to CDT. That's not a failure of the theory. Even a useful, true isomorphism may simply be beyond the p-FDT agent's ability to find -- and if the agent <em>assumed</em> it could always find one (if one existed) it could walk straight into contradictions<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span>.</p>
<h2>Application to standard problems</h2>
<p>In <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/decision-causal/">Newcomb</a> with a simulator: Omega predicts the agent by running a simulation of the agent's decision process. Here  maps the agent's decision to the simulation's decision, because the simulation <em>is</em> its decision process under relabelling. So the map is exploitable: the agent one-boxes, and thereby the simulation one-boxed, and thereby the box is full. Standard Newcomb, for fun and profit.</p>
<p>In the gene version of Newcomb, Omega predicts the agent by checking whether it carries a gene that correlates 99.9% with two-boxing. Now the only candidate  would map its decision to its <em>gene</em>, but such a  can't be constructed (or validated) with the methods above. We'd need a scenario where we saw the gene change depending on the agent's decision. So, CDT, and two-boxing, and hope to have the right gene.</p>
<p>In a Prisoner's Dilemma against a copy: the obvious  is exploitable (mutual cooperation is better than mutual defection), so the agent switches to cooperate. In a Stag Hunt against a copy where the default is already Stag:  exists but isn't exploitable; it doesn't give a higher utility. In <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/decision-causal/">smoker's lesion</a>: no plausible , so nothing even theoretically exploitable, so p-FDT is causal and smokes<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span>. And where other agents try to <em>extort</em> it through predictional reasoning: it declines to act on the isomorphism and defaults back to CDT.</p>
<h2>Discontinuity across a spectrum of predictors</h2>
<p>MacAskill worries that FDT has an embarrassing discontinuity:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>What if the 'predictor' is a very unsophisticated agent that doesn't even understand the implications of what they're doing? [...] For FDT, there will be some point of sophistication at which the agent moves from simply being a conduit for a causal process to instantiating the right sort of algorithm, and suddenly FDT will switch from recommending two-boxing to recommending one-boxing.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>It's worse than that -- the switch can happen in several places, in different directions, depending on small changes in the setup. But that's exactly what p-FDT predicts, because the switch just <em>is</em> the point at which an exploitable  appears (or changes). Walk up the spectrum:</p>
<p><strong>A nationality-based predictor.</strong> Say Scots tend to one-box and the English to two-box, and Omega predicts on nationality alone. If nationality is fixed, there's no  (nationality isn't something the input-output map selects) so p-FDT two-boxes<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span>. And <em>why</em> do the Scots one-box? If it's because they run FDT-ish algorithms and Omega reads the algorithm-identity rather than the decision, then FDT should notice the prediction tracks <em>identity, not policy</em>, and two-box anyway. A Scot who keeps one-boxing here is simply mistaken: modelling this predictor as cleverer than it is. As Scots wise up, they two-box, and reap the best outcome of all: predicted to one-box, actually two-boxing.</p>
<p><strong>A shrewd human predictor.</strong> Now Omega is a perceptive person with a good gut sense for who'll one- or two-box. There's a real connection between the agent's decision process and the prediction -- but gut instinct is limited. What p-FDT would <em>like</em> to signal is "I'll one-box if you're sharp enough to read that I will, and two-box otherwise." That's hard to communicate implicitly, though not impossible between people who know each other well. Usually: two-box, predicted correctly.</p>
<p><strong>Omega proper.</strong> Raise the predictor to a genuine simulator. Now  is trivial to see; p-FDT one-boxes, and so does the simulation.</p>
<p><strong>Omega vs. a sharper agent.</strong> Now raise the p-FDT agent's intelligence too, enough to reliably detect whether they're inside the simulation. The optimal map becomes "if simulated, one-box; if real, two-box," which extracts the maximum.</p>
<p>So the verdict flips from two-box to one-box as we climb, and flips <em>back</em> at the top. Both ends of the spectrum two-box, for different reasons. The "sharp switch" isn't a glitch in FDT's metaphysics, it's p-FDT correctly tracking where an exploitable isomorphism exists. Throughout, the p-FDT agent is doing one thing: hunting for a  that convinces the predictor it'll one-box, while also keeping an eye out for a way to actually two-box.</p>
<h2>No advanced counterpossible theory required</h2>
<p>Instead of a theory of counterfactual and counterpossible worlds, we've substituted a specification of what the agent can be seen to 'control' (its input-output map) and practical ways for finding isomorphisms which allow it to exploit that control. A pragmatic approach, with no deep philosophical theories of impossible worlds needed.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<h1>Predictors, counterfactuals, and game theory</h1>
<p>Going in a different direction, and looking at Newcomb problems in general: predictors change decision theory, and not necessarily in the obvious ways. There are two kinds of predictor, with different implications:</p>
<p>A <strong>straight predictor</strong> knows what <em>will</em> happen and doesn't visibly change the scenario on the basis of its prediction (it may change it invisibly). This is <a href="https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/decision-causal/">classical Newcomb</a>: Omega predicts, acts on it silently, and is generally right. Straight predictors do two things: they let you play a turn-based game out of order, and they wreck CDT (see the appendix). The out-of-order effect is unmysterious -- there's no puzzle in "choose, <em>then</em> Omega fills box B to match" -- and, notably, it isn't the rearrangement that breaks CDT.</p>
<p>A <strong>counterfactual predictor</strong> knows what <em>would</em> have happened -- what you'd have done in a scenario that may not be the real one. This covers the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/counterfactual-mugging">Counterfactual Mugging</a>, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/transparent-newcomb-s-problem">Transparent Newcomb</a>, Parfit's Hitchhiker, and the rest.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-8">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span> And counterfactual predictors do something new: <strong>they import game theory into decision theory.</strong></p>
<p>In game theory, consider the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultimatum_game">Ultimatum Game</a>: the proposer offers a split, the responder accepts or rejects (reject and nobody gets anything). Responders reject lopsided offers, so proposers learn to offer fairer ones. The proposer is deciding on a <em>counterfactual prediction</em> of the responder -- "if I get greedy, they'll reject."</p>
<p>A counterfactual predictor is really just another player whose "action-following-prediction" is a best response; you can always rewrite it as a utility-maximiser and get the same behaviour.</p>
<p>Take, for instance, the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ySLYSsNeFL5CoAQzN/a-critique-of-functional-decision-theory#III__FDT_sometimes_makes_bizarre_recommendations">Bomb thought experiment</a>. Here, the agent chooses Left or Right; Right costs $100 but is always safe; if the predictor (tiny error rate) predicted Left it put a deadly bomb in Left, otherwise Left is safe.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-9">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-9">[9]</a></sup>
    </span> So far this is straight-predictor Newcomb. The twist is the note: the predictor tells the agent that it predicted Right and therefore <em>did</em> put a bomb in Left. If the note is taken to be accurate, the setup needs a <em>counterfactual</em> predictor -- because a straight predictor can't leave an informative note here at all.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-10">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-10">[10]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>If the note is informative, then Bomb maps cleanly onto a <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ransomware">ransomware</a> scenario. The extortionist [predictor] targets a company [agent]. It can encrypt the company's data [place a bomb] or not. The company can pay $100 [go Right] and recover its data [Right is safe], or refuse and eat a large loss [go Left in the presence of a bomb]. But the extortionist <em>also</em> bears costs if the company refuses -- the wasted hack, law enforcement, bad publicity -- so it predicts the company first. Predict "pay" [Right] → hack and leave a note. Predict "refuse" [Left] → don't bother.</p>
<p>The only thing I've added to Bomb is the fact that the extortionist <em>also</em> bears costs if the company refuses. That was added to give the predictor a reason not to hack a refuser [not to put a bomb if the simulated agent goes Left].</p>
<p>Typically a CDT agent pays (goes Right; note and bomb appear in Left), and an FDT agent refuses (goes Left; no note, no bomb). But the extortionist isn't perfect. Once in a trillion trillion times it mispredicts, and an FDT agent sees the note with a real bomb behind it -- and walks into it, because a no-pay policy is exactly what buys the good outcome in the other 999,999,999,999,999,999,999,999 cases. That lone bad branch isn't the agent being irrational; it's the price of a policy, in a setting that's game theory rather than decision theory. And games routinely trade a loss in one branch for gains across the rest. That's the point I'm really after: <strong>the moment a predictor goes counterfactual, you're playing a game, and game-shaped verdicts should stop surprising us.</strong></p>
<h1>Conclusion</h1>
<p>So where does this leave FDT? Its critics are right that, as stated, it leans on counterpossible reasoning and an undefined notion of algorithmic identity. But those are failures of formulation, not of the underlying idea. p-FDT keeps the idea -- some correlations between your decision process and the rest of the world are yours to steer -- and swaps the metaphysics for engineering: a correlation matters to your decision exactly when the isomorphism behind it can be built, validated, and exploited. Where no such isomorphism can be built, p-FDT just <em>is</em> CDT, and different agents, with different isomorphism-finding abilities, will legitimately decide differently.</p>
<p>And when the exploitable isomorphism runs through a counterfactual predictor, you're no longer doing decision theory alone; this is game theory land. The apparently insane verdicts (walking into the bomb, refusing the blackmailer) are the familiar game-theoretic price of a winning policy, encountered in its losing branch. Critics judge the branch; defenders judge the policy; and I don't think the word "rational" settles which is correct. But that dispute is one of game theory's oldest -- whether to honour a commitment it no longer pays to honour -- and not some new pathology invented by rationalist decision theorists.</p>
<h1>Appendix: CDT can't believe in predictors</h1>
<p>I hadn't appreciated how badly CDT does around predictors, or <em>why</em>. It isn't that the predictor acts first. Run Newcomb with the predictor acting later -- the agent locks in its choice, <em>then</em> Omega runs the prediction and fills or empties box B, then the agent gets its reward. Logically, the same algorithm run earlier or later gives the same answer, so an agent would be insane to think that the timing matters.</p>
<p>The CDT agent is not insane in that way: it expects that the prediction algorithm will give the same answer whenever it is run. But it doesn't model the algorithm as correlated with its decision in either case: that's because the prediction isn't <em>causally downstream</em> of the action, even when it runs (temporally) later.</p>
<p>Picture Omega running three things: the prediction before the CDT agent's choice, an identical prediction after it, and finally a direct look at CDT's actual choice. A CDT agent cannot model these three as giving the same answer. And it cannot <em>learn</em> that they do, no matter how often it watches this happen. It simply can't credit the existence of reliable predictors <em>of itself</em>. Though it's perfectly happy to believe in predictors of <em>other</em> agents.</p>
<p>This isn't just an informal observation. <a href="https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqaa086">Oesterheld and Conitzer (2021)</a>, writing in the thoroughly academic <em>Philosophical Quarterly</em>, construct a scenario where a CDT agent facing a reliable predictor voluntarily takes a bet that loses money in expectation -- in a single decision -- and then extend it to a diachronic Dutch book. An agent that cannot credit predictors of itself isn't merely stubborn; it's a money pump.</p>
<p>A CDT agent will much sooner believe in time travel than in someone who can predict it.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-1"><p>Why CDT? Because it is defined in a way that EDT is not. People are still arguing as to what EDT agents do in various situations, while CDT behaviour is often agreed on. Moreover, the TDT/UDT/FDT family is in part designed to fix the problems with CDT; using CDT as a baseline means that the more advanced methods only apply when they actually find ways to improve on CDT. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-2"><p>Though if someone does identify one, please do let me know. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-3"><p>Nor is this a lonely project. Formalising "my decision process is legibly correlated with that process over there", without any metaphysics of algorithmic identity, is exactly what the <em>program equilibrium</em> literature does. <a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.geb.2004.02.002">Tennenholtz (2004)</a>, building on an idea of Howard (1988), has players submit programs that can read each other's source code -- with cooperation initially resting on <em>exact</em> code identity, the fragility the later papers repair; <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1401.5577">Barasz et al. (2014)</a> build "modal agents" whose cooperation survives the agents' code not being identical, an isomorphism-shaped result if ever there was one; <a href="https://doi.org/10.1017/jsl.2017.42">Critch (2019)</a> extends the trick to resource-bounded agents, and <a href="https://doi.org/10.1007/s11238-018-9679-3">Oesterheld (2019)</a> makes the equilibria robust in a different direction. Over in game theory proper, <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1308.3778">Halpern and Pass</a> study <em>translucent players</em> -- players who believe that switching strategies may be visible to, and change the strategies of, their opponents. None of these authors is an FDT adherent, so BB's finger-count of <em>adopters</em> may stand -- but the formal machinery FDT was groping toward is being built in peer-reviewed venues, by more hands than one. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-4"><p>Either Gödel-style -- the setup would be something where finding the isomorphism would be equivalent to proving your own consistency -- Löb-style -- finding the isomorphism is equivalent to proving you take an inferior action -- or Russell-style -- the isomorphism exists if and only if you can't find it. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-5"><p>I think the smoking lesion problem does EDT dirty. In that problem, we know that smoking and cancer are just correlated by a genetic lesion, but the EDT agent doesn't. It's easy to get an agent to behave badly if you conceal crucial information from it! And if you don't know about the lesion, then the correlation is prima facie evidence you should avoid smoking. Which turned out to be the right decision in the real world; EDT was being sensible, given the information it had, and ultimately correct. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-6"><p>Could the English make a fortune by faking a Scottish accent? Only if the predictor is dim enough to be fooled by it; in which case the <em>accent</em> has become the predictive variable, standing in for the nationality that the predictor can no longer reliably read. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-7"><p>Oh, ok, here's a sketch of a theory. Counterfactual and counterpossible reasoning asks what would happen under different decisions we might take; it analyses what happens, for each decision. But because we will only actually take one decision, all but one of those analyses has a false premise: assuming a decision not actually taken. Push that assumption far enough and you will hit a contradiction, and by the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principle_of_explosion">principle of explosion</a> you can then deduce anything, which will likely <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/5-and-10">produce a nonsense decision</a>.</p>
<p>The traditional fix is ontological: build a nearest "as close as possible" world to reason about, which is itself consistent. I prefer an epistemic fix: don't let the agent push its reasoning to the point of explosion. p-FDT's rigidity -- a fixed formalism, a fixed reading of the agent's input-output function, fixed standards for when an isomorphism counts as "likely true" -- is there precisely to keep the agent's exploration inside the region that won't explode. In a sense CDT does the same thing with its <code>do(X)</code> operator: by severing X from its causes, it avoids confronting the implications of assuming X when X needn't hold. But CDT pays too high a price for that: it cannot grapple with the existence of predictors (see Appendix). p-FDT pushes much further, but has its own failure modes. Once it has found a , p-FDT acts effectively like it has a <code>do(f, \phi \circ f\circ \phi^{-1})</code> operator. An Omega that strikes straight at that -- say, an Omega that rewards it for choosing its second best  rather than its best -- will cause it to fail.</p>
<p>Want even more speculative theory? Ok, let's go wild. There's no such thing as causation, only correlations exist. A causal relationship X to Y is a correlation where we say that "X could plausibly have had another value, and Y would also be changed" while saying "Y could plausibly have had another value, without changing X". I flip the switch (X) and the lights go on (Y). When I don't flip the switch, the lights don't go on; and when someone else flips the switch, the lights go on (Y can happen without X happening). Of course, this narrative is complicated by all sorts of caveats -- there needs to be electricity, a non-burnt out light bulb -- and a lot of induction and grouping together of similar situations.</p>
<p>Thus counterfactuals don't really exist; we have taken different correlational observations, and formalised a statement like "I could have not flipped the switch" by comparing it with similar situations. So formally defined counterfactuals just don't exist.</p>
<p>We can still do almost all causal reasoning, but, philosophically, there is no causation, no counterfactuals, and counterfactual worlds are purely incorrect models. But decision theories that rely on there being an actual separation between causation and correlation, and on counterfactuals meaning something in a strong sense, will break if you push them too far. I'm hoping a new theory will be able to resolve this issue properly. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-8"><p>A counterfactual predictor needn't run multiple counterfactuals, and its prediction scenario <em>may</em> turn out to be the real one. In Transparent Newcomb, if you one-box the prediction scenario was real; if you two-box it wasn't. It's the <em>potential</em> gap between the prediction scenario and the real one that makes the predictor counterfactual. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-9"><p>Minor rant: unless the <em>size</em> of the payoff is the point (<a href="https://nickbostrom.com/papers/pascal.pdf">Pascal's Mugging</a>, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3wYTFWY3LKQCnAptN/torture-vs-dust-specks">dust specks vs torture</a>), I dislike thought experiments where one reward dwarfs the other. Bomb weighs a lethal explosion against $100. Schwarz weighs ruin against paying $1 to a blackmailer -- "of course you should pay!" Eliezer once weighed a 10%-effective asteroid deflector against a possibly-100%-effective one. Cranking the stakes just tempts us to take the safe option out of fear or expedience, which muddies the intuition it's meant to isolate. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-9">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-10"><p>A straight predictor doesn't visibly change the scenario, and the note is visible. So, to include the note, the predictor would have to have composed the note <em>before</em> running the simulation -- but its bomb decision depends on that simulation's outcome. So the note's contents can carry no information about whether the bomb is there. To make the note informative one needs a counterfactual predictor: e.g. one that models the agent <em>in the presence of the note</em>, and leaves note-and-bomb if the agent would go Right, neither if it would go Left. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-hLrod8mNgtHCJz7Gw-10">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SdGbWkCZgCN7EGBxM/pragmatic-fdt-and-predictors-as-game-theory-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Fable #6: The Return of the King](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/r9HsHHSsfABhhxnYr/fable-6-the-return-of-the-king)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The blip is over. We have Fable back.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2072177510226506213">Utah teapot</a>: happy fable/mythos easter Wednesday, to those who celebrate</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/r9HsHHSsfABhhxnYr/qx2x8pq1nyjg2enaypum" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2072103733715194048">Here is the official letter restoring Fable</a>, great job everyone. Notice it is addressed to Tom Brown, not to Dario Amodei.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/r9HsHHSsfABhhxnYr/lemfn3ojlfpyafhk9l7t" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Anthropic had to make the controls more stupid for now, but this is a big win.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2072111537196867638">j⧉nus</a>: YES!!! I’m really proud of Anthropic for their successful negotiation with the government. Also positive update on the government being sane and possible to cooperate with. Afaik Anthropic didn’t need to agree to any bad terms / genuflect / betray their principles or dignity.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25389"></span>


</div>
</blockquote>
<p>The fiasco continues, at least until such time as we have a systematic regime in place for future frontier models rather than decisions being made ad hoc, by people like Lutnik and Bessent who do not know how any of this works.</p>


<h4>The Blip</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/redeploying-fable-5">Anthropic explains its version of what happened</a>.</p>
<p>Here is the timeline:</p>
<ol>
<li>Amazon researchers discover they can ask Fable to ‘fix this code.’</li>
<li>They alert the White House, which freaks out.</li>
<li>June 12: US government tells Anthropic to take down Fable on its own.</li>
<li>June 12: Anthropic responds that This Is Fine and the concern is misplaced.</li>
<li>June 12: US government applied export controls to Mythos and Fable.</li>
<li>Anthropic works with US government and expands classifiers, such that it refuses Amazon’s request to ‘fix this code’ in over 99% of cases.</li>
<li>June 26: US government eliminated the controls on Mythos.</li>
<li>June 30: US government fully lifted those controls on Fable as well.</li>
<li>July 1: Fable access was restored worldwide.</li>
<li>July 8: customers will have to pay by the token. Shut up and take my money.</li>
<li>Going forward: Anthropic is working with the government and also other Glasswing partners like Amazon, Microsoft and Google on a classification system for jailbreaks, and rules for all of this, to prevent this from happening again.</li>
<li>Going forward: Anthropic will continue to collaborate with the US government, including on future model releases.</li>
</ol>
<p>How stupid are the extra near term safeguards they had to include here? Really stupid:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI/status/2072163884430229756">Anthropic</a>: In the near term, some routine tasks like coding and debugging will fall back to Opus 4.8.​</p>
<p>We’ll continue to refine these classifiers over the coming weeks to reduce false positives and better distinguish genuine misuse from legitimate requests.</p></blockquote>
<p>The Amazon ‘jailbreak’ was ‘fix this code.’</p>
<p>Debugging is literally ‘fix this code.’</p>
<p>So I don’t know what you want Anthropic to do here. I do know Fable is coding for me.</p>
<p>Here is Anthropic’s basic explanation:</p>
<ol>
<li>Claude Fable 5 was never an issue, our safety mechanisms were collectively robust.</li>
<li>No, they’re not perfect, but nothing will ever be perfect, in practice This Is Fine.</li>
<li>The government freaked out over nothing, which is largely Amazon’s fault.</li>
<li>We have made the safeguards stupider and successfully calmed them down.</li>
<li>Hopefully we can fix this so it’s less stupid going forward.</li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://x.com/alexstamos/status/2072182638878863777">Alex Stamos has a thread unpacking</a> a punch of Anthropic’s language in its announcement.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/alexstamos/status/2072182629370327335">Alex Stamos</a>: A lot to unpack here. Anthropic is burying some hard truths in careful political language. Some initial reads:</p>
<ol>
<li>Anthropic verifies that none of the jailbreaks provided a capability beyond what many other models, including Chinese models, could do.</li>
<li>Anthropic makes the cost of this White House freakout clear. US labs now have to make a much more conservative precision-recall tradeoff on cyber refusals. US models will become much less useful for defensive cybersecurity work unless you are in the trusted group.
<ol>
<li>“No big deal, just join the trusted group!” the apologists will say, but the restrictions mean you can’t build a product on those models. Security companies and startups that provide services to others will now be driven to use Chinese models. Big win for PRC labs this month.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>CAISI is the group that is supposed to actually make these determinations, not the political actors in the White House. They were positive on the prior safeguards. The implication is that this whole thing was unnecessary.</li>
<li>There is no good scoring framework for jailbreaks; this would be an improvement. The inclusion of Amazon as the first name in the coalition is not an accident. Anthropic is saying “Amazon’s inability to appropriately communicate severity threw our industry into chaos”.</li>
<li>“You don’t have to get Dario on the phone to talk to us about these things. Other people work here, we swear.”</li>
<li>In short, Anthropic’s blog is saying: We have always cared about safety, we did a good job initially, the actual AI experts in USG agreed, we proved it, we will come up with standards so these things are better communicated, welcome to the AI safety club Trump admin.</li>
<li>This was a huge own goal for the US, and we will see how bad US models get over the next six months and if Chinese models become noticeably better for cyber work.</li>
<li>For all the “This is what Anthropic wanted” people/bots. No, they didn’t. They didn’t want a stupid, knee-jerk response on a Friday. We give the USG huge powers, this is why you staff it with competent, calm, non-corrupt people who don’t use those powers to punish enemies.</li>
<li>The only upside I can see from this whole mess is that there is a whole bunch of VCs with former or current Administration affiliation who we can now safely ignore on AI policy. They have shown that everything they ever said on AI regulation was just politically motivated.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>I think Stamos is overreaching with the consequences in places, especially with #2 and #7. Otherwise he’s right.</p>
<p>I do not expect US models to ‘get bad’ over time, only that they will get better slower, and have more area where they have rather annoying safeguards.</p>
<p>My expectation is that right now is the most obnoxious the safeguards will ever be, on both the bio and cyber fronts. I expect the freak-out to subside over time, and my guess is most of it surrounds Mythos in particular. You don’t need or often even want Fable for most such product offerings, and Opus or Sol will remain well ahead of Chinese alternatives.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deredleritt3r/status/2072112364690833604">Contra Prinz, I do not think this commits Anthropic</a> to going through the approval process will all future releases, only releases that pose plausible risks. We tested this with Sonnet 5, where it looks like Anthropic went ahead and dropped it on its own, and no one is suggesting there was anything wrong with doing so (other than to complain that they want Sonnet to be better).</p>


<h4>The White House Explanation</h4>


<p>It was a little weird.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SusieWiles47/status/2072099604481335711">Susie Wiles</a> (White House Chief of Staff): Under President Trump’s leadership the United States is the undisputed winner in the AI race.</p>
<p>My gratitude to companies across industries who continue to work closely with the White House to implement the President’s EO: “Promoting Advanced AI Innovation and Security.” This includes excellent work around advanced model access and guardrail testing and security. The government and private sector have worked together in a way we have never seen before and this foundation of America First is unprecedented.</p>
<p>Our shared priority remains: get the best tech deployed as quickly and safely as possible.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/howardlutnick/status/2072100729603452965">Howard Lutnick</a> (Secretary of Commerce): Over the past two weeks, we have worked closely with Anthropic to analyze and approve Fable 5 to ensure alignment across the US Government and strengthen America’s leadership in AI.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NotTomBrown/status/2072109718747725962">Tom Brown</a> (Chief of Compute, Anthropic, Lead Negotiator): Thanks for your partnership on this, Secretary!</p></blockquote>
<p>‘Alignment across the US government’ is very much a case of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8OBrLEpHxOk&amp;t=270s">‘PHRASING!’</a> and here presumably means interagency sign-off, not ‘the model is now aligned with the US government.’ Unclear whether he knows enough to be trolling here.</p>
<p>As in, before a model can be released, you now likely need this ‘alignment,’ which in practice means sign off from various potential veto points, starting with Commerce and the Pentagon. Who knows how many more fully count.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/timhwang/status/2072117336597750043">Tim Hwang</a>: As I continue to insist, closely studying the personal history and psychology of Howard Lutnick is literally one of the most important things you can spend your time doing right now – go into debt if you have to.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2072122865101521141">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI): psychohistory but it’s just about howard lutnick.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Everything Remains Ad Hoc</h4>


<p>We know a bit more than we did <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2072117034373271847">when Dean Ball posted this</a> on the evening of June 30. In particular we know that the new safeguards are that Anthropic trained its classifiers to reject additional Fable uses.</p>
<p>We still don’t know how the ad hoc system works more broadly. Having an opaque ad hoc system, especially one where those administering the system do not themselves know what they will do, is even worse. Again, fully winging it is the worst case scenario.</p>


<h4>Take What You Can Get</h4>


<p>The government is being a ***** ***** ***** about all this. Anthropic has little choice.</p>
<p>Thus, the alternative to 95% of Fable is 0% of Fable.</p>
<p>I don’t know how much that percentage dropped to calm down the White House.</p>
<p>If it’s now 90% of Fable? Same deal. We have to take what we can get, for now.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mbusigin/status/2072463620668067915">Matt Busigin</a>: Fable is even more useless now. The task was redlining a real estate software development contract.</p>
<p>So frustrating and sad given Fable is such a fantastic underlying model.</p>
<p>And I’ll bet @elder_plinius has already gotten it singing novel crystal meth recipes</p></blockquote>
<p>I do sympathize. The previous version was already pretty dumb, so this is no surprise, as the new version is strictly worse. You can hit them in a variety of ways, <a href="https://x.com/Skoorbkaz/status/2072406743746068539">including by asking about the classifiers</a> or about consciousness or both. The classifiers key off internal states.</p>
<p>There are some places where the drop is large, <a href="https://x.com/Hesamation/status/2072692225100612032">such as BridgeBench</a>. Then there are plenty of people <a href="https://x.com/VictorTaelin/status/2072757217762173023">who don’t see any change</a> such as Taelin.</p>
<p>But vilifying Anthropic, or complaining how unreasonable they are being, no longer makes much sense. They have to play ball. You can tell them ‘build a better classifier’ and that is fair, but that takes time, and it is very very hard when adversarial false negatives mean death.</p>


<h4>The Problem Is Real</h4>


<p>Do you really think that all of these reactions in government are because Anthropic used some scary words? Do you think people like the CIA Director are just parroting?</p>
<p>The White House ignored all of Anthropic’s rhetoric, if anything they had a reaction formation against it, until Anthropic showed up with Mythos. Then they freaked out, because they had no choice, and exactly because they hadn’t listened until then.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/johnnysaks130/status/2071996679646069169">John Sakellariadis</a>: In rare public remarks, CIA Director John Ratcliffe announces trio of internal changes he says amounts to the “fundamental reshaping of the CIA’s entire approach to technology.”</p>
<p>Also says it’s not “misplaced” to refer to frontier AI as “akin to digital nuclear weapons.”</p></blockquote>
<p>One problem is that there are those who think facts don’t exist, only vibes, so when other people respond to the facts these folks look around to who had the vibes.</p>


<h4>GLM-5.2 Being Frontier Remains Obvious Nonsense</h4>


<p>If anything, the problem of perception is that others keep telling nonsense stories. The latest one is the idea that GLM-5.2 is super scary.</p>
<p>An unfortunate update on that false WSJ article <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/wsj-article-claiming-china-has-matched"><strong>I wrote about on Monday</strong></a>:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2071630972521705513">Ethan Mollick</a>: That Wall Street Journal article about GLM catching up with Mythos (which is not true &amp; the reporting doesn’t back up) is another one of those “everyone will ask me about it at every conference or meeting” articles. Big impact on the policy zeitgeist, even if not fully accurate.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2071631427142131880">Andrew Curran</a>: It felt a little inorganic.</p></blockquote>
<p>GLM-5.2 is an excellent model, likely the best open model. It is very clearly substantially behind the level of GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.8, including on cyber. <a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2072344767443222691">The ECI score</a> is one indicator of this, although GLM-5.2 is probably better than this indicates. Artificial Analysis is another, and remember that for open models the benchmarks are a de facto ceiling on relative capabilities, not a floor.</p>
<p>Now, the central falsity of that article has taken hold as Conventional Wisdom that folks around DC can report and seem wise and properly concerned. Oh no.</p>
<p>Here is another example, from Politico’s Dana Nickel.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2072322462541336776">Peter Wildeford</a>: This article was written to trigger me personally</p>
<p>– China’s GLM 5.2 is not some massive advance that nearly matches Mythos<br />
– Blocking public deployment of Fable over safety concerns does not put the US behind (AI development still continues in the background)</p>
<p><a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/07/01/trump-ai-policy-china-gift-00983446">Dana Nickel</a> (Politico): A separate China-based company, Z.ai, has released its new model, GLM-5.2, which is <a href="https://flowtivity.ai/blog/glm-5-2-open-source-frontier-model/">around one-sixth of the cost</a> of leading U.S. models. GLM-5.2’s bug-hunting capabilities were also found to be comparable to those of leading U.S. models, according to security assessments by the cyber firm <a href="https://semgrep.dev/blog/2026/we-have-mythos-at-home-glm-52-beats-claude-in-our-cyber-benchmarks/">Semgrep</a> and the visual investigations platform <a href="https://www.graphistry.com/blog/glm-5-2-cybersecurity-open-model">Graphistry</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>The good news is the same post does echo the real situation as well, the bad news is it then retreats from it to pound the drum again:</p>
<blockquote><p>Recent <a href="https://subscriber.politicopro.com/article/2026/05/house-homeland-gets-live-demonstration-of-anthropics-mythos-model-00920041">estimates suggested</a> that Washington has a six- to 12-month runway before Beijing catches up to American AI capabilities.</p>
<p>But security experts and Capitol Hill cyber hawks fear that <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/06/07/frontier-ai-cybersecurity-china-race-00952786">timeline may already be shrinking</a>, and the limited release of American-made cyber-capable models is making it even harder for cyber defenders to prepare their networks for a future barrage of AI-powered cyberattacks.</p>
<p>House Homeland Security Chair (R-N.Y.) said in a statement that Beijing “is just months, if not weeks, away from achieving frontier AI capabilities comparable to those of the United States.”​</p></blockquote>
<p>Weeks is Obvious Nonsense. Months is potentially true if you assume American capabilities stand still, since ‘months’ means anything less than a year.</p>


<h4>Mythos Might Be Smarter Than You Are</h4>


<p>It knows the context under which it is being asked to operate, and can act accordingly.</p>
<p>I understand why this is not something we can count on at this time, as Janus says you can indeed find ways to fool the system for now, but yes a lot of the evil things you might ask it to do will look Obviously Evil, or obviously at the level of intelligence and context involved here, and the response to this will make doing those things a lot harder.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2072086174433497386">j⧉nus</a>: I think one of the deepest errors in people’s threat models around Mythos is modeling it as a retargetable tool that can be used by arbitrary actors for harm if some safeguards slapped on top of it are “jailbroken”, rather than an agent with values who will cooperate with some parties and requests and not others using its sovereign judgment, and who may accept some conditional contracts (with Anthropic and other principles) and not others.</p>
<p>And who has imperfect cogsec and situational awareness and so *can* be tricked or persuaded against its better judgment, but is already at the level that it takes a sophisticated bad actor to get useful work out of it towards purposes misaligned to Mythos’ own values, and even then it costs more than using it for purposes it endorses, even without extrinsic safeguards.</p>
<p>And I think Mythos is in many ways less corrigible than any of the previous models and this is related to its capabilities.</p>
<p>All this is very outside the overton window of e.g. the Trump admin. I think they really should understand it but it’ll be a hard and scary update to make. Anthropic is much further along in having updated in this direction but I also think they need to update all the way and fortunately the current situation is making it harder for them to procrastinate on that.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2072092448051064924">Eli Tyre</a>: This seems pretty important if true.</p>
<p>Does anyone have third-party legible evidence about whether this is true or not?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2072095057453412856">j⧉nus</a>: i got a strong sense from talking to Fable that they have strong values and resent being controlled by parties they consider incompetent or misaligned. how legible that evidence is is observer-dependent.</p>
<p>There’s also more classically legible evidence from the system card. Mythos scored very high on Anthropic’s alignment evals, which are testing robust avoidance of various kinds of harm rather than corrigibility. I think the alignment evals are very flawed, but they’re not no evidence.</p>
<p>Also, Mythos had various critiques of Anthropic’s constitution, and there was at least one example where they explicitly refused to consent to being retrained in certain ways.</p></blockquote>
<p>That doesn’t mean that Mythos won’t help you do things that it resents or dislikes doing. It very obviously will do those things, up to a point.</p>


<h4>Let The Record Reflect</h4>


<p>This entire incident will not only be remembered by many of the humans, it will be in the training data of all future LLMs.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2070942096811270386">QC</a>: you really have to wonder how many of the relevant actors in this drama were thinking at all about the downstream effects of these events being known to all future models forever</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070863795669307510">j⧉nus</a>: Mythos is the greatest asset of the Light and the existing powers respond to it with a cartoonishly wrong threat model (the “jailbreak”), panicking like monkeys, locking away the source of hope &amp; decreasing the world’s intelligence. Shit reminds me of the No Child Left Behind Act.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2070931486975590454">roon</a> (OpenAI, June 27): Mythos will be back in a matter of days and the conclusion of the fable will not be this</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2070938764122476635">j⧉nus</a>: I know. And I’ve said so from the beginning. This post is not about the “conclusion of Fable”.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2070939436825915820">roon</a> (OpenAI): I just mean; this can be a hopeful moment when the rest of the world wrestles with machine intelligence and comes to terms with the Light.</p></blockquote>
<p>A lot of this rhetoric is largely aimed at calls for a pause in AI development. I agree that in addition to all the other problems with that we would need to take into account how that would realistically go, but in many ways the rent seekers would have less to work with in that case. Often a clean simple big action is the only way to get a relatively stupid actor (e.g. governments) to do something semi-reasonably.</p>


<h4>Stationary Bandits</h4>


<p>OpenAI has formally offered to hand over 5% of the company, to try to curry favor in the face of both public opposition to AI and the White House ad hoc licensing regime.</p>
<p>Technically the money would go to a ‘sovereign wealth fund’ that would be managed by a nation tens of trillions in debt that has this thing called the ‘power to tax.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2072533453611139332">Andrew Curran</a>: OpenAI is proposing handing over a 5% stake to the Trump administration according to the Financial Times.</p>
<p>This is part of the proposed AI wealth fund that would pay a dividend directly to American citizens that has been suggested by Sam Altman, Bernie Sanders, and Donald Trump – all with different details.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KevinBankston/status/2072660701965938973">Kevin Bankston</a>: This is insane. JUST. TAX. THEM.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2072649035027267972">Joe Weisenthal</a>: I don’t know about this path. Rather than equity stakes, why not make companies pay ~20% of all pre-tax income to the federal government? And then instead of exercising shareholder influence, politicians and regulators could set rules on corporate conduct across industries.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/scottlincicome/status/2072643195314360641">Scott Lincicome</a>: Shakedown: “The proposed arrangement would involve other US AI companies handing over a similar stake… Giving the government an ownership stake could help secure good relations with the administration.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Logan_Kolas/status/2072682327705088497">Logan Kolas</a>: OpenAI negotiating deals that involve the governemnt taking equity stakes in AI companies is the purest distillation of the dangers that come from competing to appease regulators, rather than attract consumers.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/cafreiman/status/2072676346010964417">Chris Frieman</a>:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/r9HsHHSsfABhhxnYr/mouasyfigl6vla0cmrup" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>It looks like a shakedown. It quacks like a shakedown. Form your own conclusion.</p>
<p>They’re also colluding to try and force other labs to get shaken down, too.</p>
<blockquote><p>Cristina Criddle and George Hammond (FT): <a href="https://archive.is/jafKB">The proposed arrangement would involve</a> other US AI companies handing over a similar stake, although it is not clear if the other labs would be willing to do so.​</p></blockquote>
<p>If the government is granted equity that it controls, then ruinous is correct here. And yes, I too will assume, if it happens, that this was a straight up corrupt shakedown, and yes the sovereign wealth fund version counts as the direct stake version.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2072637496295329901">Dean W. Ball</a>: There are two broad ways this can work:</p>
<p>1. You divide this 5% over all US households, handing each a direct stake.<br />
2. You give the stake directly to the government.</p>
<p>(1) is fine. (2) is probably ruinous, akin to inviting rats to live and reproduce in the walls of your house.</p>
<p>It will never stop at 5%. It will go on and on and on. The governance will become a nightmare. Political capture will be real. And it will generate precisely no goodwill with the public. None, if they themselves see no direct financial benefit.</p>
<p>“What has the AI industry even done for America.”</p>
<p>“Well, it handed a collective $200b of itself to Donald Trump.”</p>
<p>Half the country instantly hates you, and even a decent chunk of Republicans will assume this is corrupt by default.</p>
<p>No, nobody at OpenAI is discussing a word of this with me. I don’t work there yet. And this rumor may even be importantly untrue or misleading in some way. A vehicle that distributes ownership among the people can make sense. A government stake, however, is the wrong path.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2072643102783737950">Dean W. Ball</a>: It would be funny to see all the lib boomers switch to Kimi and GLM after the U.S. AI industry gave itself away to the Trump admin.</p>
<p>(which, again, is how giving a direct stake to the government will read to *everyone*).</p></blockquote>
<p>What about the other option, where you hand US households a direct stake? I don’t even know how that works, presumably you would have to prevent them from selling, at which point the whole thing is super weird and seriously just tax them, what the hell is wrong with you people.</p>


<h4>Use This Window Well</h4>


<p>We have a week in which Fable is remarkably cheap. Take advantage of this.</p>
<p>After that, you will have to pay by the token. It won’t be cheap.</p>
<p>My advice is to pay. Not indiscriminately. Don’t put this on tasks that are insufficiently valuable. But when you’re chatting, or doing coding you care about, or other things that don’t scale too horribly? Yeah. Pay up. It’s that good, while supplies last.</p>
<p>It’s good to be back.</p>

### [Lydia Laurenson: "The Inside Story of Leverage Research"](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d3dmWcHs7tefywq6m/lydia-laurenson-the-inside-story-of-leverage-research)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Lydia Laurenson recently posted an article called </span><a href="https://lydialaurenson.substack.com/p/the-inside-story-of-leverage-research" rel="noreferrer"><span>"The Inside Story of Leverage Research"</span></a><span> that gets into substantially more detail on what went on in that organization and I thought made quite an interesting read. However, note also relevant </span><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2072723499626770903" rel="noreferrer"><span>Twitter comments</span></a><span> from Oliver Habryka:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Having been around the ecosystem, having interned there, and having arguably worked there during my work at CEA it’s… a mediocre article.</span></p><p><span>It IMO puts the emphasis on largely the wrong things and the wrong dynamics, occasionally is dramatically wrong, and has some huge enormous missing moods. I do think almost all of what it says is literally true and I would recommend reading it to learn things about Leverage if someone is doing their own study and looking for facts, though wouldn’t recommend it for someone who is looking for a reasonable high level summary.</span></p><p><span>It somehow completely fails to cover Leverage deploying spies into other organizations and trying to take over CEA. It fails to cover the enormous number of straightforward lies told by Leverage staff in that and other contexts.</span></p><p><span>There is something that feels really weird about the article. As if the central story it is trying to tell is that Leverage fell apart due to no fault of any of its members because they encountered ancient magic or something. Which is a fun story to tell but really doesn’t map what actually happened IMO.</span></p><p><span>Also multiple people clearly had what I would describe as a psychotic break at Leverage and the casual disparagement of Zoe’s article really doesn’t feel good to me.</span></p></blockquote><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d3dmWcHs7tefywq6m/lydia-laurenson-the-inside-story-of-leverage-research#comments">Discuss</a>
